Solche Spaltungen für eigene Zwecke auszunutzen und zu vertiefen, ist die Strategie „Teile und Herrsche“.  Ein proaktives Mittel, um eigene Interessen auf Kosten anderer voranzutreiben besteht darin einige zu bevorzugen (die Macht der Begünstigten zu erhöhen) auf Kosten anderer (die Macht der Geächteten zu verringern).

Für die spaltende Macht spielt es keine Rolle, wie die Spaltung umgesetzt wird, oder wie bestehende Spaltungen vertieft werden, oder wer aus welchen Gründen auch immer hilft, oder ob diejenigen, die die Spaltung begünstigen und daraus Vorteile ziehen es überhaupt wissen, dass sie die Spaltung unterstützen: Was zählt, ist, dass es umgesetzt wird. After Napoleon's catastrophic Russian campaign in 1812, where his army suffered massive losses due to harsh winter conditions, prolonged battles, and Russia's "scorched earth" tactics, he was already significantly weakened.
By the time of the Battle of Leipzig in 1813, also known as the "Battle of Nations", the French army had lost much of its strength. Seeing this opportunity, Prussia joined the Sixth Coalition, alongside Russia, Austria, and Sweden, to defeat Napoleon. The battle lasted from October 16 to October 19, 1813, and resulted in a crushing loss for Napoleon. This forced him to retreat to France, ultimately leading to his abdication in 1814 and exile to Elba.
This was a turning point in European history, as Prussia solidified its position as a major power. Though Napoleon would later return for his final attempt at reclaiming power during the Hundred Days in 1815, he was ultimately defeated at Waterloo.

>  1. Amos 5:26  
> “You shall take up Sikkuth your king, and Kaiwan your star-god—your images that you made for yourselves.”  
> (ESV)  
>   
> Context:  
> Amos is condemning idolatry among Israelites during a time of national corruption. He's not speaking against Jews as a people group, but rather against specific sinful practices (namely, syncretism and idol worship).  
>    
> The critique is internal, directed at the people of Israel from within the covenant community. The prophets often rebuked their own people out of love and a call to repentance. It is theological, not ethnic: the issue is idolatry, not ethnicity.  
>   
> 2. Acts 7:43  
> “You took up the tent of Moloch, and the star of your god Rephan, the images that you made to worship; and I will send you into exile beyond Babylon.”  
> (Stephen quoting Amos during his speech before the Sanhedrin)  
>    
> Stephen, a Jewish follower of Jesus, is addressing Jewish leaders, summarizing Israel’s history of rejecting God's messengers, culminating in the rejection of Jesus. Stephen is Jewish himself, and like the prophets before him, he rebukes covenant unfaithfulness.  
>   
> 3. Revelation 2:9  /  4. Revelation 3:9  
> “I know your tribulation and your poverty (but you are rich) and the slander of those who say that they are Jews and are not, but are a synagogue of Satan.”  
>    
> “Behold, I will make those of the synagogue of Satan who say that they are Jews and are not, but lie—behold, I will make them come and bow down before your feet, and they will learn that I have loved you.”  
>   
> These are letters to Christian churches in Asia Minor (Smyrna and Philadelphia). Jesus (through John) is encouraging persecuted Christians, many of whom were being opposed by hostile local Jewish groups who rejected Jesus as the Messiah and denounced believers to Roman authorities.  
>   
> The term "Jews who are not" refers to spiritual inconsistency, not to a denial of Jewish ethnicity. The phrase "synagogue of Satan" is a figurative, prophetic rebuke to those opposing the Gospel, not a condemnation of all Jews.

> Perhaps you're confusing "German" with Germanic? Habsburg Austria wasn't "German" untill AH "unified" it with the 3.R., Habsburg Austria was a major Power Player and "Archenemy" of Prussia, they constantly conquered each others lands. The theoretically "overlord" within the "Holy Roman Empire" would have been the "Kaiser" (from Caesar, Imperator), who was without any power at this time. This "Holy Roman Empire" wasn't German (for Germany foundet itself afterwards). For Example, western part of Habsburg Austria and all those small German speaking roams (from Prussia down to Bavaria) were part of it. German speaking Switzerland was not, they got out by rebellion pretty early. On the other hand, this Holy Roman Empire included parts of nowadays Italy, Bohemia, Tschechien, Poland, Hungary, usw... . It wasn't German, for that didn't exist at this time. It included a lot of German speakers, which at this time identfied themself as "subjects" of their local roam.

Habsburgs policys of "divide and conquer / rule" are well known, but you have to put them in context with the other power fractions, Prussia, Saxony, Bohemia, Russia, England, France, Italian Free Citys, the Pope in Rome, Katholik League, Protestant League, and so on. > That is not correct. The issue lies in a lack of familiarity with key historians such as Brigitte Hamann or Theodor Mommsen. Before 1867, there was no Hungarian state in the modern sense—only the Austrian Empire. After Austria was forced to withdraw from German affairs following the Austro-Prussian War of 1866 (sometimes referred to as the “German War”), it became necessary to establish the Ausgleich (“Compromise”) of 1867. This agreement created a dual monarchy: the Austrian Empire was joined by the Kingdom of Hungary as a separate, semi-autonomous entity—what Robert Musil famously described as “one egg with two yolks.”  
>   
> From that point onward, the entity was known as Austria-Hungary. The German-speaking population within the empire became a minority, which contributed to the rise of nationalist and völkisch movements, such as those surrounding Georg von Schönerer, whose writings were later read by Adolf Hitler.  
>   
> The war of 1866 was indeed a German conflict. Prussia’s unilateral withdrawal from the German Confederation was a breach of legal norms and constituted a form of secession. In response, Austria sought to defend the Confederation, with military support from other German states such as Bavaria, Hesse-Darmstadt, and Baden.  
>   
> German history during this period was far more complex than commonly assumed. The Kleindeutsche Lösung (“small German solution”)—which excluded Austria from German unification—was by no means universally accepted. Austria’s expulsion from German affairs was famously lamented by the Austrian poet Franz Grillparzer, who wrote: "Ihr glaubt, ihr habt ein Reich geboren, und habt doch nur ein Volk zerstört." “You think you have given birth to an empire, but you have only destroyed a people.” So: no 1866, no Hitler.

Just a Small tip:
Everytime you walk into the frame, the Camera (set to automatic) adjusts it’s shutter speed, which as a consequence starts showing the flickering of the screen. So if you would fix the shutter speed permanently to a certain value, and only make the aperture or just the ISO variable to control the exposure, you can get rid of the flickering in your footage.

Besides, great content!

Claims that German emigration to colonies was possible ignore the fact that Germany’s short-lived colonial empire (1884–1918) offered minimal long-term migratory outlets compared to British or French colonies, and white Americans with German ancestry largely descend from earlier 18th–19th century immigration, not colonial efforts.   
>   
> The assertion that Nazi Germany didn’t turn to paganism is equally false, Heinrich Himmler explicitly modeled the SS as a pagan priesthood, replaced Christian rites with solstice celebrations, and transformed Wewelsburg Castle into a mythic Aryan temple. To pretend that Nazi ideology stayed within Christian bounds is historically false and ideologically naïve.

> The idea that Nietzsche’s rupture with Wagner was driven by moral disgust over antisemitism is not only wrong, it’s historically absurd. Nietzsche was not some enlightened 19th-century anti-racist. In fact, while he often mocked organized antisemitism (especially the populist movements of his day), he frequently indulged in racial stereotypes himself.  
>   
> In Jenseits von Gut und Böse (Aphorismus 195), Nietzsche blames the Jewish “slave-morality” for reversing the natural hierarchies of strength and valor:  
>   
> “Die Juden  ein Volk ‘geboren zur Sklaverei’ mit ihm beginnt der Sklaven‑Aufstand der Moral.”  ￼  
>   
> While he also praises certain Jewish contributions to European morality in the same work, this framing remains rooted in racial essentialism, not genuine philosemitism.  
>   
> In Der Antichrist, Nietzsche lambasts the moral influence of Judaism and Christianity as a corrupting “priest-caste,” urging a kind of spiritual purge:  
>   
> Priestersystem sollen „verfehmen, aushungern, in jede Art Wüste treiben“.    
>   
> His break with Wagner had far more to do with Wagner’s embrace of Christian morality in Parsifal, the growing kitsch of his aesthetics, and Nietzsche’s own ballooning ego, not some principled defense of Jews. The claim that Nietzsche was an early moral crusader against racism is a comforting myth created by the post war American Allied powers, especially by the sponsorship of Walter Kaufmann, not a fact. His record proves otherwise.

Bismarck’s social reforms weren’t spawned by fear of the SPD; they were rooted in his conservative Prussian-Christian ethos, expressed long before the SPD had real influence. In his 1881 Kaiserliche Botschaft (Imperial Message), written by Bismarck himself, he declared:  
>   
> “Die Heilung des sozialen Übels ist nicht in der Unterdrückung, sondern in einer positiven Förderung des Wohles der Arbeiter zu suchen.”  
> (“The remedy for social problems lies not in repression, but in the positive promotion of workers’ welfare.”)  
> That came before the SPD had more than token Reichstag presence.

> His Krankenversicherungsgesetz (Health Insurance Act, 1883), Unfallversicherungsgesetz (Accident Insurance Act, 1884), and Invaliden- und Altersversicherungsgesetz (Old Age and Disability Insurance Act, 1889) laid the foundation of the world’s first welfare state no socialist country, Britain or France did this first. And this wasn’t just legislation, Bismarck saw it as a tool for Staatsbindung (binding workers to the state), writing in 1884:  
>   
>   
> “Ich halte es für möglich, den arbeitenden Klassen den berechtigten Anteil a den Kulturgütern zu sichern, ohne dass sie zur Sozialdemokratie überlaufen.”  
> (“I believe it possible to secure for the working classes their rightful share of culture’s benefits without their turning to social democracy.”)

### **@MarkusWitthaut** | 👍 70 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o2Nq--qU9Kc&lc=UgxOvIHyHtDiFj1gpB14AaABAg)

Thanks for the video. I have some issues with statement made right at the beginning (first five minutes):
1. The region mentioned as Königsberg is actually the northern part of a former German province called Ostpreußen (East Prussia) which was divided after WWII and annexed by the Soviet union. Kaliningrad is both the new name of the city formerly named Königsberg as well as the name of the region (oblast Kaliningrad) which was created after WWII. BTW, the southern part of East Prussia now belongs to Poland.
2. Prussia was never for a couple of hundred years the most powerful country in Europe, let only the world. Prussia only became a strong European power after the Napoleonic wars  (1815). But even then the British Empire and the French Empire were far more powerful. Only after the unification of Germany in 1871, then Germany became the dominating power in continental Europe for some decades.
3. The former German province West Prussia is now not a part of Germany. Instead it is belonging since 1919 to Poland. 
4. Königsberg was the major city in East Prussia but never the center of a Prussian, let alone German civilization. This was Berlin. Königsberg was very important for the Deutscher Orden (Teutonic Order) that ruled the Baltics. 

I don't know if this continues in the remainder of the video (I decided to stop listening), but I would strongly advise to check the statements made here.

> Don't add milk to green tea or earl grey, milk is best with regular black tea blends like "English breakfast" or "Assam". The names of different types of tea are based on where the tea leaves were cultivated and how the leaves were processed, which changes the flavour profile and chemical composition of the leaves. Green tea is the least "processed" form of the tea leaf, and should be enjoyed without any additional ingredients. To acquire a taste for green tea, I started with a very small dose of leaves in the brewing process, so I ended up with subtly flavoured hot water. Then I increased the amount each time until I hit the sweet spot. Now I've always got a hot beverage by my side when working.

### **@derter** | 👍 12 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o2Nq--qU9Kc&lc=UgzjHaIJJgPypf0k8ht4AaABAg)

Firsf of all prop for being such an enthusiastic teacher that makes what it looks like really interesting classes.

Now as a German student majoring in politics and history I have quite a few confusions and problems with this video.
I will list some 

1- First of all you explain a lot of German history and culture using quotes, ideas and statements from people of the past. 
That is actually a good idea but the lack of reflection and clarification of those outdated ideas really bugs me.

The prime example for that is Hitlers quote from January 30, 1939 (1:01:50)
Here you deciffer well Hitler's idea that the Jews pose the biggest threat to Germany.
Representing capitalism and comunism, what he deams the biggest danger to his facism, now we know that this was just lies and propaganda fed to the German people to unite them against an enemy, obviously all lies are build on slivers of truth (here being that there were some rich capitalist Jews and some communist ones) 
My problem is not that you use this quote from Hitler, thus it's important to understand the mindset of that time, but that you leave it unchecked and without clarification now that we know that it was bullshit.

2- Second, staying in the same part of the video: 
Wtf do you mean we don't know if the Holocaust happened???
There is A LOT OF EVIDENCE that the Holocaust happened and you just bluntly denying it is quite shocking, and shaking my view of you as an history and political science teacher.

3- This is minor but still important if you want to produce a representative history of Germany.
Prussia was never the lone hegemon of Europe, neither the biggest power of earth.
The statement that prussiar culture and people were for some hundred years superior to others is nonsense, and prussias military supremacy were also not even close to that time span.

4- You often make assumptions and connections between topics that are not related.
An example would be that Nietzsche thought his Übermensch as a superior form of race that would be better then others, something the Nazis used as an excuse and example for why the Aryan race is better. 
The thing is Nietzsche never meant that, his idea of an Übermensch was rather that of the best version of ourselfs and that for us to reach that we would need to outgrow ourselfs and be better than we thought we could be.



There are multiple other problems pointed out in other comments so please go and read them.

My History teacher, Professor "Rohl" spent an entire year persuading his tutor group that WW1 was caused entirely by Germany.
His great 'slamdunk' item of evidence came from an original source dating to the 1890s ... 
He'd unearthed an original first hand account by a contemporary  german cabinet minister in the 1890s who had written about a cabinet meeting where they discussed expanding the naval harbours. 
This was to accommodate Dreadnaught class of battle ships. 

(which in turn would in turn be taken as a clear provocation to the UK, leading to the naval and military arms-race of the early 1900s)

What Professor Rohl had ommitted however from his account was the final sentence of this source :  

"The meeting amoubted to nothing." 

As far as i am aware there's still no evidence Germany was planning for war in the 90s or  00s.

Voltaire's statement was actually not so unfounded, since Prussia was more or less the state (1230-1561) of the Teutonic Order (a monastic and knightly order founded around 1190 and confirmed by the Pope in February 1199, operating under the same rules as the Templar and Hospitaller Orders).

The Order's state was based on monastic principles on a larger scale, the experience of the civil service apparatus in Syria and Byzantium, and the administrative experience of the Norman Kingdom of Sicily. This "monastic and knightly culture" had a cultural and political impact well into the 20th century.

### **@otto1449** | 👍 1 likes

One could argue that the military prowess often attributed to the German army was, in part, the result of institutionalizing Napoleon’s art of war. After Prussia’s crushing defeat in 1806, military reformers such as Scharnhorst and Gneisenau studied Napoleon’s methods closely. They recognized the effectiveness of his operational agility, strategic innovation, and ability to mobilize an entire nation for war. These insights were codified and gradually implemented through reforms – most notably the creation of a professional General Staff – which later became the cornerstone of German military excellence in the 19th and 20th centuries.

Poland existed as a state long before the Kingdom of Prussia became a major power, with the Polish state forming in the 10th century and its kingdom established by 1025, while the Prussian state (as a duchy from Teutonic Order remnants) emerged in 1525, and Prussia as a kingdom later in 1701, with Poland later being partitioned by Prussia and other empires in the late 18th century. As Poland existed as a state for centuries before the entity known as "Prussia" emerged as a major power, and Polish nationalism in the 19th century was heavily influenced by the loss of their independent nation during the Partitions, nationalism helped to resist forced germanisation.

Évidemment, les simplifications sont nécessaires à des fins pédagogiques, mais il ne faut pas sous-estimer l'interprétation de la culture et de la pensée européenne à tout moment de l'histoire. A titre d'exemple, Frédéric le Grand était parfaitement francophone et francophile et plus tard Napoléon III était parfaitement germanophone et parlait français avec un fort accent allemand. Ce juste pour illustrer que même dans les oppositions de pouvoir des dirigeants des Etats européens, les interférences de pensées étaient constantes et se copiaient rapidement. Les révolutions étaient donc souvent concommitantes (ex 1948), les guerres aussi hélas, mais l'évolution du droit également : la loi sur les sociétés commerciales de 1866 en France s'inspire largement des récentes lois allemandes. Les circonstances et les hommes ont fait souvent la différence et les conflits. Napoléon I était par exemple un Européen concaincu qui voulait rationaliser l'Europe par  un système administratif commun (il créait des prefectures au fur et a mesure de ses conquêtes, comme Maastrich aux Pays Bas, ou même Hambourg. et la Grande   Armee comptait pres d'un tiers  d'Allemands) quand plus tard Bismarck était un Prussien militariste brutal qui a mené la guerre pour soumettre les autres Etats allemands par la force.  Il n'avait qu'une vision nationaliste étroite de l'histoire,  et est largement à l'origine des causes des deux guerres mondiales. Rappelons que, du point de vue linguistique, l'unité linguistique des Etats  est récente car en fait il y avait partout une mosaïque de langues et de patois.

This is what it means for Jesus to declare himself the Son of God because he doesn't need someone else to hand him the keys to the kingdom he _is_ the unity of will. He _is_ God. Not in the sense that he is all powerful but that he doesn't need to seek the unity of will outside himself like most people and can therefore lead instead of following.

It's not evne necessarily the case that some people have the capacity to manifest the unity of will that most lack, but that some are more predisposed to that charismatic mode. All people fundementally have the unity of will within them and that is in fact the basis of what makes the conformity -- the "unity" part of "unity of will" -- possible. Anyone who thinks they are pre-destined to this role will surely grow arrogant and ruin not only themselves but the world.

### **@clemclem4317** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o2Nq--qU9Kc&lc=UgwK0MfL-vOgSBWpv8h4AaABAg)

Honestly, it’s fascinating how much easier it is to present yourself as an iconoclast than to do the hard, unglamorous work of real scholarship. You keep circling back to Prussia and Königsberg as if mythologizing “lost greatness” can substitute for actual historical analysis. Yes, Königsberg produced Kant and some brilliant minds, but calling it the epicenter of the Enlightenment—over Paris, London, or Vienna—is pure rhetorical overreach.

The real history of Prussia and Germany is a lot messier than your simplified hero/victim narrative. Prussian militarism wasn’t some reluctant accident; it was baked into the state’s DNA for centuries. And blaming “the West” for its destruction—while ignoring the overwhelming reality of the Soviet advance and the broader catastrophe of WWII—isn’t just misleading, it’s intellectually lazy.

You keep invoking “feudalism,” global bankers, and secret conspiracies, as if complexity can be collapsed into a single villain. Actual banking and power structures are complicated, competitive, and often in tension—not some shadowy, unified cabal. If you’re going to argue the big stuff, at least respect your audience enough to offer nuance, not just vibe.

But what really jumps out is how much performance there is here. The constant posturing, the dramatic pauses, the fixation on appearing “deep”—it’s a tell. There’s a real difference between genuine curiosity and using history as a stage for ego. Wanting to feel strong or important by mythologizing “strength” is understandable, but at the end of the day, it’s just hiding insecurity behind rhetoric.

You don’t have to borrow the myths of vanished empires to have worth. Real strength comes from wrestling with uncomfortable facts, and real originality is in seeing clearly—not in selling the illusion of secret knowledge.

1. Poland was not a Lutheran community at all.

6. East Prussia was the first Protestant state in history.
